outlier/label contamination. It compared the current quadric SVD path, full and
diagonal covariance, Ledoit-Wolf shrinkage, Minimum Covariance Determinant,
low-rank-plus-diagonal covariance, and one-component GMM covariance.

Selected mean results:

| Dimension | Quadric SVD accuracy | Full covariance accuracy | Shrinkage accuracy | Full covariance fit time |
| --------: | -------------------: | -----------------------: | -----------------: | -----------------------: |
|         3 |               90.44% |                   99.19% |             98.09% |                   0.2 ms |
|         6 |               99.31% |                   99.31% |             98.04% |                   0.3 ms |
|         9 |               99.41% |                   99.41% |             98.03% |                   0.2 ms |
|        12 |               99.17% |                   99.17% |             97.75% |                   0.3 ms |
|        19 |               99.41% |                   99.41% |             97.50% |                   0.3 ms |

All fitters completed all seeds. From dimension 6 onward, the current SVD path
and full covariance produce effectively identical results, consistent with the
current SVD implementation falling back to covariance when the fitted quadric
is not positive definite. Full covariance lies on the accuracy/runtime frontier
for this condition. MCD is robust but slow (about 79-88 ms) and does not improve
held-out accuracy; diagonal and rank-5 covariance lose anisotropic structure.

The follow-up robustness sweep separated clean data, 25% unconstrained
outliers, 20% mislabeled-shell noise, 50% component overlap, and support sizes
of 40 and 100 at dimensions 9 and 19. Key results were:

- shrinkage led clean, overlap, and low-support conditions, including 93.56%
  balanced accuracy at `d=19, n=40` versus 67.55% for full covariance;
- the quadric path cannot fit below its 54-point (`d=9`) or 209-point (`d=19`)
  minimum, while covariance methods remain available;
- MCD was the only robust method under 25% unconstrained outliers, scoring
  96.06% at `d=9` and 94.21% at `d=19`; full covariance fell to 52.02% and
  50.00%;
- full/GMM covariance remained strongest under mislabeled-shell noise.

Matched candidate-count and 0.1-second wall-clock comparisons preserved the
conditional frontier. At `d=19` with 25% outliers, MCD reached 91.93% in the
wall-clock comparison while the other methods remained near 50%. At `d=9`,
cheap full-covariance search attempted about 136 candidates and reached 92.33%,
beating MCD's 81.07%. On clean `d=19`, shrinkage reached 98.80%.

The synthetic gate therefore advances shrinkage and MCD as the two non-default
candidates for Tier 4 screening, with the current full-covariance path retained
as comparator. This is a conditional frontier, not evidence for a universal
replacement. Test samples were observational and were not used to choose
candidates. No production default has changed.

Tier 4 screening used five seeds, one common transform per seed, and disjoint
geometry, selection, calibration, and test slices. Shrinkage beat the current
fitter on four selection slices and tied one, with a mean multinomial gain of
+0.75 pp and pooled paired 95% interval `[+0.17, +1.37]` pp. Its observational
test gain was +0.71 pp. However, it averaged 106 additive primitives versus
47.2 and 1.00 seconds versus 0.48 seconds. MCD generated no plausible Tier 4
models and was eliminated before Tier 5.

The one locked Tier 5 confirmation used 15,000 CIFAR-100 samples and seed 42.
The current fitter won the selection slice, 67.67% to 66.11% multinomial
accuracy, a 1.56 pp margin. Observational test accuracy was effectively tied at
66.13% versus 66.10%. Shrinkage used 186 primitives versus 42, took 1.91 seconds
versus 0.59 seconds, and left three classes empty. Therefore M5 retains the
current fitter as the production default. The opt-in fitter interface remains
available for future domain-specific robustness studies.

During confirmation, an exact-boundary defect in kNN seeding was found and
fixed: a pool containing exactly `k` points now returns the full pool instead
of calling `argpartition` with an out-of-bounds index.

## M6: temporal representations (periodic toy gate)

The first M6 implementation adds a causal hybrid representation containing an
exact one-hot character window and a deterministic reservoir state evaluated
at the window boundary. Alignment and future-mutation tests verify that targets
remain strictly future. The previously declared `synthetic_periodic` dataset is
now implemented as an offline deterministic corpus.

One locked periodic toy run compared exact window, single reservoir, and hybrid
representations using identical sample, transform, geometry, and forward-split
budgets. Selection used forward-validation accuracy only.

| Representation   | Forward validation | Observational test | Linear control | n-gram |
| ---------------- | -----------------: | -----------------: | -------------: | -----: |
| Exact window     |             49.33% |             47.22% |         88.89% | 93.23% |
| Single reservoir |             83.33% |             83.89% |         96.67% | 93.23% |
| Hybrid           |             53.33% |             60.00% |        100.00% | 93.23% |

The reservoir advances from this toy condition. Hybrid's perfect linear score
but weak GEODE score indicates that the combined state contains the signal but
the current low-dimensional geometry/readout pipeline does not exploit it.

The variable-order gate compared single, multi-timescale, multi-seed, and
hybrid reservoirs at fixed total width. Initial GEODE forward accuracy remained
low (15.74-16.64%). Train-only linear tuning selected a 24-dimensional hybrid
with recurrence 0.95 at 39.65%, but equal-geometry GEODE screening favored the
24-dimensional single reservoir, 22.03% versus 18.14%. This divergence again
identified geometry/readout extraction as the limiting stage.

On a 100k-character WikiText slice, exact context beat the selected reservoir
on forward validation, 19.42% versus 15.59%. A train-only 12k-character tuning
prefix then selected an exact five-character window at 37.29% linear forward
accuracy. No test sequence was used by the tuner.

The one locked 50k/10k confirmation produced:

| Method                   | Top-1 accuracy |
| ------------------------ | -------------: |
| GEODE, calibrated        |         30.36% |
| Feature-logistic control |         34.64% |
| Matched-data 5-gram      |         44.50% |
| Best-practical 5-gram    |         47.61% |
| Unigram                  |         19.22% |

GEODE forward-validation accuracy was 27.57% +/- 0.98, raw uncalibrated test
accuracy was 10.15%, calibrated top-5 was 64.59%, and perplexity was 14.25.
Under the predeclared decision gate, Tier 6 remains evidence that the common
GEODE architecture can consume causal temporal representations, not evidence
of competitive text prediction. The test result did not select or tune the
representation.

M6.7 then froze one 50k/10k sample set and ordered, disjoint geometry,
carve-acceptance, calibration, forward-validation, and final-test segments.
The same additive model seeded all variants, refinement used geometry samples
only, subtraction forked from the additive model, and every variant refit its
calibrator on the same calibration segment.

| Variant        | Validation | Test (observational) | Test NLL | Test ECE | Subtractive primitives |
| -------------- | ---------: | -------------------: | -------: | -------: | ---------------------: |
| R0 additive    |     27.21% |               27.05% |    2.776 |    0.045 |                      0 |
| R1 refinement  |     28.51% |               27.90% |    2.761 |    0.038 |                      0 |
| R2 refinement  |     29.00% |               27.90% |    2.754 |    0.038 |                      0 |
| S1 subtraction |     27.47% |               26.72% |    2.798 |    0.053 |                    106 |

R2 advanced with +1.79 pp validation and +0.85 pp final-test accuracy over R0.
S1 did not: its small validation gain failed test confirmation, model size grew
substantially, and fit time rose from 29.3 to 86.5 seconds. Supervised
refinement is now explicitly named as such in source and result schemas;
subtraction remains disabled by default.

## M7: OOD, calibration, and selective prediction

M7 used CIFAR-10 as the in-distribution dataset. Geometry, calibration, ID
validation, and original CIFAR-10 test samples were disjoint. OOD validation
selected each 95%-TPR operating threshold; final OOD test samples were
observational only. The real-family run used one frozen CIFAR-10 model and
transform for both CIFAR-100 and SVHN, with 1,066 validation and 1,343 final
test examples per family, matched to the corresponding ID counts.

The controlled radial feature-shift gate produced:

| Shift distance | Raw SDF AUROC / FPR95 | Metric SDF AUROC / FPR95 | Mahalanobis AUROC / FPR95 | k-NN AUROC / FPR95 |
| -------------: | --------------------: | -----------------------: | ------------------------: | -----------------: |
|            4.0 |         0.976 / 0.100 |            0.995 / 0.008 |             0.999 / 0.008 |      1.000 / 0.000 |
|            6.0 |         1.000 / 0.000 |            1.000 / 0.000 |             1.000 / 0.000 |      1.000 / 0.000 |
|            8.0 |         1.000 / 0.000 |            1.000 / 0.000 |             1.000 / 0.000 |      1.000 / 0.000 |

First-order metric correction materially improved the near-shift SDF score,
but did not beat the matched distance baselines. Maximum calibrated probability
became anti-informative as radial distance increased, falling from AUROC 0.733
at distance 4 to 0.430 at distance 8.

Final balanced real-family test results were:

| OOD score                       | CIFAR-100 AUROC | CIFAR-100 FPR95 | SVHN AUROC | SVHN FPR95 |
| ------------------------------- | --------------: | --------------: | ---------: | ---------: |
| Minimum raw SDF                 |           0.552 |           0.899 |      0.337 |      0.937 |
| Minimum metric SDF              |           0.555 |           0.916 |      0.343 |      0.955 |
| SDF energy                      |           0.412 |           0.959 |      0.153 |      0.990 |
| Maximum-probability uncertainty |           0.786 |           0.579 |      0.831 |      0.431 |
| Mahalanobis                     |           0.232 |           0.992 |      0.082 |      0.998 |
| GMM negative log-likelihood     |           0.516 |           0.946 |      0.297 |      0.952 |
| k-NN feature distance           |           0.475 |           0.946 |      0.243 |      0.966 |

On the untouched CIFAR-10 test set, calibrated GEODE accuracy was 82.28%, NLL
was 0.544, Brier score was 0.260, and ECE was 0.0245. The nominal 90%
split-conformal sets achieved 88.61% coverage with average set size 1.19.
Risk-coverage and accuracy-rejection arrays are retained in the machine-readable
artifact.

Maximum-probability uncertainty beat matched Mahalanobis and k-NN ranking on
both real OOD families, satisfying the relative M7 exit gate. However, FPR95 of
0.579 on CIFAR-100 and 0.431 on SVHN is too high for a reliable abstention or
deployment-grade rejection claim. The supported conclusion is limited to
better relative OOD ranking on these two families; geometry-native SDF and
energy scores did not transfer from controlled radial shift to natural-image
shift.

## M8: robustness and editability

The robustness harness applies deterministic corruption only to the shared
training slice. Calibration and clean test data remain fixed and are never used
for fitting. The one-seed smoke matrix covered symmetric label noise at 5%, 10%,
and 20%; 20% class-conditional noise; 10% feature outliers at distances 3, 6,
and 10; 20% and 50% missing dimensions; and covariance-shift strengths 0.3 and
0.7. Every corruption records its affected rows or dimensions.

The smoke study exposed a hard missing-dimension limitation: masking one of six
training dimensions left all four GEODE classes empty and reduced clean-test
accuracy from 87.75% to 25.00%. Masking three dimensions produced the same
failure. This is construction failure rather than gradual predictive
degradation; the current full-quadric fitter does not tolerate a zero-variance
training dimension.

The predeclared feature-outlier hypothesis advanced to five seeds. Mean clean
accuracy and mean changes under 10% outliers were:

| Method                      | Clean accuracy | Distance 3 change | Distance 6 change | Distance 10 change |
| --------------------------- | -------------: | ----------------: | ----------------: | -----------------: |
| GEODE                       |         87.25% |          -1.38 pp |          -1.68 pp |           -2.78 pp |
| Shrinkage Gaussian          |         88.72% |          -0.58 pp |          -1.38 pp |           -8.95 pp |
| Matched GMM                 |         79.47% |          -0.90 pp |          +3.50 pp |           +5.23 pp |
| k-NN                        |         86.13% |          -0.10 pp |          -0.45 pp |           -0.20 pp |
| Linear SVM                  |         87.60% |          +0.20 pp |          -0.95 pp |           -0.83 pp |
| Histogram gradient boosting |         85.72% |          -1.63 pp |          -1.60 pp |           -0.45 pp |

At distance 10, GEODE retained all classes across all five seeds and degraded
less than the shrinkage Gaussian, supporting a conditional advantage over that
covariance baseline. It degraded more than k-NN, linear SVM, boosting, and
matched GMM, so the broad hypothesis that RANSAC geometry is the most robust
method under feature outliers is rejected.

The five-seed primitive-stability study held data and 800 evaluation points
fixed while varying only construction seeds `[11, 23, 37, 53, 71]`. Total
additive component counts ranged from 25 to 30 (mean 27.2, variance 3.76).
Hungarian matching to seed 11 gave mean center drift 0.392 and mean relative
precision drift 0.581, while mean prediction agreement remained 93.0%. The
aggregate decision function is substantially more stable than the individual
primitive identities; claims that a particular fitted ellipsoid is a stable
explanatory unit are therefore not supported. Carve-region overlap is not
applicable to this additive-only stability protocol.

The online-editing exit gate used a fixed three-class model and 2,401-point
grid. A point-fitted insertion into class 0 changed 329 predictions, deleting
that primitive reversed all 329 changes, and structured rollback restored both
the exact serialized snapshot and all baseline predictions. A two-iteration
class-local nudge changed 186 predictions. Insertion, deletion, and nudge left
the other two classes' SDF arrays bitwise identical. The audit records measured
75 microseconds for point fitting plus insertion, 2 microseconds for deletion,
57 microseconds for rollback, and 421 microseconds for nudge in this toy run;
these timings establish low-overhead operation on the tested model, not a
scaling claim. M8 is complete: editing is local, auditable, and reversible, but
broad corruption robustness and seed-stable primitive identity are rejected.

## M9: editability scaling

M9 uses a locked one-factor-at-a-time synthetic protocol. Starting from an
8-class, 8-dimensional model with 8 primitives per class, it independently
sweeps class count, dimensionality, and primitives per class from small to 128.
Each condition has five timing repeats, 256 fixed observational evaluation
points, and 24 update points. It records insertion, deletion, rollback, local
nudge, cache invalidation, and inference latency; canonical serialized model
size; changed predictions; and bitwise stability of every untouched class.

Target-class and full-model reconstruction are included as scaling comparators.
They reconstruct deterministic synthetic structures and must not be described
as optimizer retraining. All locality and rollback gates passed in the locked
five-repeat run. Median point-fitted insertion remained between 34 and 177
microseconds. Exhaustive inference rose from 0.27 ms at 2 classes to 15.89 ms
at 128 classes and from 0.13 ms at 1 primitive per class to 14.81 ms at 128.
The operation counters confirm the cause: class-candidate pairs scale with class
count and primitive-SDF pairs scale with total primitive count.

Dimension growth exposed a different bottleneck. At 128 dimensions, 64 dense
orientation primitives serialized to 4.32 MiB; median rollback was 99.36 ms and
local nudge was 102.15 ms, versus 0.34 ms and 1.13 ms at 2 dimensions. M9 is
complete for the declared synthetic range. Local attachment is cheap, but
exhaustive inference and full dense snapshots do not support unlimited scaling.

## M10: calibrated open-set inference

Immutable empirical support profiles are now separate from role fingerprints.
Opt-in model and orchestrator paths return explicit `UNKNOWN` decisions, frozen
threshold margins, profile versions, and routing-stage counters without changing
closed-set APIs. Seven focused contract tests and the leave-class-out protocol
test passed.

In the first six-class toy episode, classes 0-2 were known, class 3 alone was
used for score/threshold selection, and classes 4-5 remained final observational
unknowns. Mahalanobis and k-NN were perfect on the proxy class but their frozen
thresholds rejected only 2.5% and 3.75% of final unknowns. Minimum SDF rejected
36.25% while retaining 99.17% known coverage; maximum probability rejected all
unknowns but retained only 1.67% of known samples. This is negative evidence for
single-proxy threshold transfer.

The locked three-episode study kept proxy classes 3-5 globally disjoint from
final unknown classes 6-8 and completed seven score families, global versus
per-class thresholds, proxy-recall versus known-coverage operating points, and
matched additive versus validation-gated CSG geometry. No cell passed the
predeclared 90% known-coverage / 50% unknown-recall gate in every episode.
Known-coverage-constrained metric SDF was strongest globally, with 88.3%
minimum test coverage and 27.5% minimum unknown recall. CSG accepted zero
subtractive carves and was identical to additive geometry. M10 contracts remain
available for experimental opt-in inference, but this study does not support a
production profile or automatic class-discovery claim.

## M11: automatic unlabeled-group discovery and safe adaptation

### Goal pivot from unknown classification to unknown-group clustering

From M11.22 onward, the primary discovery goal is to automatically partition
accumulated weakly supported observations into distinct, persistent unlabeled
groups. If repeated car and bicycle observations are both unsupported, the
system should form two groups from their embeddings without knowing either
semantic name and without being told the number of groups in advance.

Group formation is autonomous: support ranking, buffering, persistence,
clustering, duplicate suppression, and stable review-ID creation cannot inspect
oracle or user labels. Human feedback occurs only after a group exists and may
name it, map it to an existing class, mark it invalid or irrelevant, or leave it
unresolved. Automatic clustering therefore does not imply automatic semantic
classification, class creation, or model mutation. Any confirmed adaptation
still requires the existing usefulness, replay, OOD, calibration, graph, and
rollback gates.

The bounded rejection buffer, delayed-label four-event stream, and non-mutating
proposal gate are implemented. The initial five-seed comparison showed that
clustering alone was unsafe: DBSCAN averaged 50.7% discovery precision and
76.7% recall with four false proposals, while incremental centroids averaged
68.6% precision and 29.2% recall with two false proposals. Delayed oracle
analysis identified new modes of existing classes as the main false-growth
source.

A development-only sweep selected minimum known-class separation before five
disjoint holdout seeds were observed. Frozen DBSCAN separation 2.5 achieved
100% precision, 99.2% mean recall, 95.8% minimum recall, and zero false
proposals across all five holdout streams. Proposals first appeared in window 1,
not the initial window, satisfying the persistence gate. This remains synthetic
evidence: update/create actions, permanent semantic names, model mutation, and
LLM proposals are still disabled.

The first action-policy iteration uses isolated nearest-mode surrogate
candidates, not GEODE edits. Across five new seeds it evaluated seven confirmed
proposals: two existing-class updates and five new-class creations. The gated
policy matched all seven confirmations with no measured known-replay accuracy
or OOD-recall regression. Always-update and always-create controls reached only
28.6% and 71.4% action accuracy. This validates confirmation and gate plumbing,
not the safety of changing a `FittedModel`: all seven candidates required
confirmation, `mutation_published` remained false, and transactional GEODE
update plus versioned graph-migration tests are still pending.

The transactional GEODE iteration replaced surrogate candidates with actual
ellipsoid experts. Existing-class actions execute inside isolated
`ModelEditor` transactions with exact snapshot rollback; new-class actions
version the source output classes and fingerprint and validate a copied graph.
Across the same five seeds, all five confirmed new-class migrations gained
62-77% proposal coverage while preserving known replay accuracy and 100% OOD
recall. Both confirmed existing-class updates were quarantined: replay accuracy
fell by 10 and 15 percentage points, and one also missed the 50% proposal-gain
gate. Decision accuracy was therefore 71.4% against confirmation, correctly
favoring safety over action matching. No mutation was published. Calibrator
refitting and nontrivial downstream reconstruction are now exercised by a
separate calibrated graph-migration study. Across five seeds, each copied
graph refit its three-column source calibrator on calibration-only data,
reconstructed a downstream node for the expanded score input, passed graph
validation, and executed with 100% observational accuracy on the deliberately
separable toy. Every live graph remained unchanged and no migration was
published. Broader streams and the failed M10 production gate remain blockers
to automatic class creation.

The frozen DBSCAN policy was next evaluated without retuning on heavy-tailed,
anisotropic, abrupt-drift, and intermittent-unseen streams over five new seeds
per family. It emitted zero false proposals in all 20 runs, but proposed in
only four of five runs per family. Mean recall ranged from 73.8% to 79.2%, and
minimum recall was zero. The common miss was seed 63: its unseen centroid was
2.34 units from the nearest known centroid, below the frozen 2.5 separation
gate. This is conservative rather than accidental, but shows that fixed
geometric separation alone cannot resolve a nearby new class from a new mode
of a known class. The negative result blocks automatic creation and motivates
evidence beyond centroid distance; the policy was not changed after holdout
observation.

The ambiguity-resolution iteration preserves the frozen separation gate but
retains clusters that fail only separation under stable, non-actionable review
IDs. Across the same 20 family/seed runs, review plus proposal coverage raised
mean unseen recall to 92.5-97.5% and minimum recall to 81.2-95.8%, at a cost of
9-11 reviewed clusters per family. Delayed confirmation identified 38
existing-class modes and four nearby new-class clusters. The unchanged
proposal-gain, replay, OOD, and transaction gates permitted 37 isolated
updates and four isolated creates and quarantined one update whose 47% gain
missed the frozen 50% requirement. No review was treated as an automatic
proposal and no mutation was published. These are surrogate action results;
nearby-class GEODE transactions and real confirmation workflows remain open.

Replacing the review surrogates with actual GEODE ellipsoid transactions
produced a strong negative transfer result. All 38 confirmed existing-mode
updates were rolled back because replay accuracy fell by 2.1-20.8 percentage
points; 30 also missed the 50% proposal-gain gate. Three of four nearby-class
migrations passed with 64-77% proposal gain and no replay or OOD loss. The
intermittent-unseen create reached only 47% gain and was quarantined. Thus 39
of 42 reviewed actions were quarantined, four transactions were safety
accepted, only three matched the confirmed action, and zero mutations were
published. The low 7.1% confirmation-action match is expected safety behavior,
not a routing target. Existing-mode GEODE updates require a less destructive
local fitting method before publication can be considered.

The constrained fitting iteration first required every other-class replay
point to remain outside the candidate ellipsoid by a fixed SDF margin. That
constraint had zero violations but did not reduce replay loss because no
candidate needed to shrink. The actual cause was top-level normalized softmin:
adding a distant disconnected expert changes the normalization denominator and
raises the target-class score even where the new expert is irrelevant. The
corrected representation uses hard union across independently fitted class
modes while retaining normalized softmin within each smooth expert. Combined
with replay exclusions, this accepted 24 of 38 existing-mode updates with zero
replay or OOD loss; all 14 remaining updates failed only the frozen proposal-
gain gate. The three previously accepted creates remained accepted, the
low-gain intermittent create remained quarantined, and no mutation was
published. This resolves the synthetic replay regression mechanism but still
requires real-feature and calibrated-graph validation.

The calibrated mode-update iteration applies hard union to a copied source
graph, refits its unchanged-width two-column calibrator on a disjoint
calibration slice, reconstructs and recalibrates a direct downstream node, and
executes held-out observations. All five transactions and graph validations
passed; source, downstream, old-mode, and new-mode accuracy were 100% on the
deliberately separable toy. Every live graph remained unchanged and no update
was published. This resolves the calibrated DAG mechanics only; real-feature
difficulty remains the next required test.

The real-feature iteration uses disjoint CIFAR-10 source images, HOG features,
a PCA/scaler fitted only on live geometry, and a horizontal-flip mode for one
class. An initial run exposed and corrected a protocol defect: candidate
calibration had a class-prior imbalance and reused source images across mode
views. Under the corrected balanced, source-disjoint protocol, all three
candidate ellipsoids had zero exclusion violations but only 3-16% proposal
coverage after shrinking to 55-70% radius scale, so every transaction was
quarantined. The live model already recognized the flipped mode at 84.2% mean
accuracy. No mutation was published. This negative result shows that
persistence and confirmation do not imply a useful compact GEODE mode in a
fixed real-feature representation.

The representation-aware usefulness gate now checks two necessary conditions
before opening a transaction: the baseline must leave enough theoretical gain
headroom to satisfy the frozen 50% gain requirement, and the exclusion-safe
ellipsoid must cover at least 50% of confirmed proposal samples in the current
feature space. All three CIFAR-10 candidates failed both criteria, so zero
transactions and zero recalibrations were attempted. Candidate predictions
therefore remained exactly equal to live predictions, with zero old-mode,
new-mode, or other-class change. Replay/OOD transaction gates remain mandatory
for candidates that pass this cheaper usefulness screen.

The frozen screen was then transferred without retuning across horizontal
flip, brightness, Gaussian noise, and center-occlusion modes over three seeds.
All 12 candidates failed gain headroom because live mode accuracy was already
83-88%; nine also failed geometric coverage. Gaussian-noise proposals were
comparatively compact at 65% mean coverage, but still could not possibly meet
the frozen 50% gain requirement. The benchmark is explicitly pre-transaction
because it lacks a frozen real OOD control pool: zero transactions,
recalibrations, or mutations were attempted.

Harder transfer then evaluated cat/dog, deer/horse, and bird/frog pairs across
three shifts and three seeds, with all eight excluded CIFAR classes retained
as a disjoint OOD control pool. Mean live mode accuracy fell to 51.2%, so 13
of 27 cells had enough gain headroom, but no cell also met the frozen 50%
geometric-coverage requirement. Conversely, the few compact candidates lacked
gain headroom. No threshold was retuned and no transaction was attempted. Raw
GEODE sign rejection identified only 3.3% of excluded-class controls as
unknown, with pair means of 1-6%. This independently confirms the failed M10
open-set production gate on real features: calibrated real OOD rejection must
precede any transactional real-feature study.

Development-only real-feature OOD calibration is now complete across three
known-class pairs and three seeds. Each cell used source-disjoint HOG slices,
fit PCA/scaling only on known geometry, selected thresholds at 90% known
coverage, used CIFAR classes 6/7 only as proxy unknowns, and reserved classes
8/9 as untouched final unknowns. Ten score families were compared, including
raw and metric SDF, SDF energy, calibrated maximum probability, global and
class-conditional Mahalanobis, GMM likelihoods, exact k-NN distance, and a
class-conditional Weibull-tail control. Development selected maximum
probability, but final known coverage averaged 88.8% and unknown recall only
11.4%, with a 5.0% minimum and zero of nine cells passing the frozen 90%/50%
gate. Metric SDF had the best observational final recall at 22.2% but also
passed zero cells; conformal sets rejected none of the untouched unknowns.
This broad negative result rules out threshold substitution as the immediate
fix. Real mutation remains disabled, and the next gate is a source-disjoint
representation adequacy comparison under the same OOD protocol.

The frozen representation comparison is now complete for HOG, pretrained
MobileNetV2, and deterministic 8x8 pooled RGB over the same 27 total cells.
Every representation reused the M11.18 source-identity rule, proxy/final class
pools, seeds, score-family selection rule, and production gate. MobileNetV2
materially improved the representation: closed-set accuracy rose from 68.6%
to 94.2%, neighborhood purity from 0.625 to 0.823, and the compactness ratio
fell from 2.62 to 1.52. Its development-selected maximum-probability score
reached 39.4% mean final unknown recall versus 11.4% for HOG. The improvement
did not transfer uniformly: minimum recall was 25.0%, minimum known coverage
was 82.5%, and only one of nine MobileNet cells passed. HOG and pooled RGB
passed zero cells. No representation therefore passed the advance rule, no
mutation was attempted, and the next experiment must audit the support
objective on the frozen MobileNet representation rather than select another
representation from final results.

The support-objective audit is now complete. Ten MobileNet score components
were exposed only through an opt-in diagnostics payload. Within each of the
nine frozen cells, known-validation and proxy-unknown samples were split into
disjoint fit and model-selection halves. Global and predicted-class
conditional logistic support calibrators were compared at C values 0.01, 0.1,
1, and 10. Development selected the global C=1 model, which reached 43.1% mean
proxy recall and passed four of nine development cells. After refitting on all
development samples, untouched transfer reached only 85.0% mean known
coverage, 36.9% mean unknown recall, 15.0% minimum recall, and zero passing
cells. This is worse than the frozen maximum-probability baseline at 39.4%
recall and one passing cell. Class-conditional candidates never exceeded two
development cells. Linear score ensembling and predicted-class conditioning
are therefore rejected as immediate fixes. Real mutation remains disabled.

The frozen larger-sample stability study is now complete. Each disjoint slice
was expanded from 40 to 100 samples while MobileNetV2, maximum probability,
global logistic C=1, class pools, seeds, thresholds, and production gates
remained fixed. Maximum probability recovered 90.4% mean known coverage, with
a 95% cell-bootstrap interval of 88.5-92.2%, but unknown recall remained only
36.8% (31.6-42.1%), the minimum was 23.0%, and zero of nine cells passed. The
frozen ensemble degraded to 88.6% coverage and 28.5% recall, also with zero
passing cells. Baseline AUROC ranged from 0.692 to 0.861, showing useful rank
signal but no threshold that consistently satisfies both constraints. The
larger study therefore rejects finite-sample threshold quantization as the
primary blocker and identifies persistent near-OOD support overlap. More
calibration samples alone are not an adequate fix; external outlier-exposure
controls are next, with final CIFAR classes still untouched for selection.

M11.22 pivots the operational goal from per-point unknown classification to
automatic discovery of persistent unlabeled groups within accumulated support
problems. A backward-compatible policy mode now emits stable review IDs for
coherent groups while remaining non-actionable and emitting no temporary
semantic class IDs. The real-feature study
selected a 20% flag budget using proxy classes 6/7 only, then froze it for all
nine MobileNet cells with untouched classes 8/9. Event recall reached 100%,
useful-review precision 94.4%, mean time to review one window, and review
burden 3.89 per 1,000 observations. However, fixed DBSCAN usually merged the
two unseen categories: mean distinct-group recall was 11.1%, adjusted Rand
index 0.039, cluster purity 59.1%, and duplicate-review attempts 68.1%. Thus
repeated rank evidence is sufficient to surface a support problem for review,
but the current clustering geometry does not support a claim that distinct
unknown categories are recovered. No semantic IDs, class creations,
transactions, or mutations were produced. All 81 infrastructure tests passed.

M11.23 tested whether M11.22's category merging was caused by a fixed DBSCAN
scale. Flag fractions 5-30% and epsilon values 0.5-2.0 were compared using
proxy classes 6/7 only, prioritizing distinct-group recall and adjusted Rand
index before event recall and review burden. Development selected 30% flagging
and epsilon 1.0. On untouched classes 8/9, distinct-group recall improved from
11.1% to 61.1%, mean cluster purity from 59.1% to 79.9%, and adjusted Rand
index from 0.039 to 0.250. The tradeoff was 88.9% event recall, 300 flags and
7.5 reviews per 1,000 observations, and 59.0% expired flags. Only a subset of
cells recovered both unseen groups, so density-scale selection establishes
partial unlabeled group recovery but not reliable category separation. Final
labels did not select the budget or scale, and no mutation was published.

M11.24 tested whether variable density, rather than representation overlap,
caused the remaining merge/fragmentation failures. The matched development
grid compared DBSCAN against HDBSCAN without supplying the number of unknown
groups. Proxy classes selected HDBSCAN with 30% flagging, minimum cluster size
3, 66.7% development distinct-group recall, and 0.434 adjusted Rand index. On
untouched classes 8/9, event recall reached 100% and useful-review precision
97.8%, but distinct-group recall fell to 44.4%, adjusted Rand index to 0.163,
and cluster purity to 69.9%. This is below M11.23's tuned DBSCAN result of
61.1% distinct-group recall and 0.250 adjusted Rand index. Hierarchical density
clustering therefore does not resolve the transfer failure; the next test must
separate clustering-space information loss from clustering-algorithm choice.
No labels informed grouping, no group count was supplied, and no mutation was
published. All 82 infrastructure tests passed.

M11.25 held HDBSCAN and maximum-probability support ranking fixed while
comparing the 8D known-class PCA geometry against L2-normalized raw MobileNet
features. Proxy-only selection chose raw MobileNet space with 30% flagging and
minimum cluster size 3; development adjusted Rand index reached 0.636. On
untouched classes 8/9, adjusted Rand index improved from M11.24's 0.163 to
0.301, but distinct-group recall was only 38.9%, cluster purity 75.0%, and
useful-review precision 74.1%. Event recall remained 100%. The richer
representation therefore preserves more partition information but does not
produce reliable autonomous groups under the current online review lifecycle.
This iteration also exposed that group metrics use first-review snapshots
rather than the final accumulated partition; that lifecycle/evaluation defect
must be corrected before another representation conclusion. No labels informed
grouping, no group count was supplied, and no mutation was published.

M11.26 corrects the evaluation boundary to match the operational goal. Online
review delay and burden still use first detection, but autonomous group quality
is now measured by reclustering the complete flagged buffer after all windows;
all flagged unknown points, including clustering noise, remain in adjusted Rand
index. Proxy-only selection again chose normalized raw MobileNet features,
HDBSCAN minimum size 3, and 30% flagging. On untouched classes 8/9, accumulated
event recall was 100%, distinct-group recall 61.1%, and final cluster purity
79.8%. Four of nine cells recovered both categories, three recovered one, and
two recovered neither. Mean unknown-majority group count was 2.33, but adjusted
Rand index fell to 0.059 once noise and fragmentation were counted. Accumulation
therefore reliably detects recurring unsupported events and sometimes recovers
the two desired unlabeled groups, but does not yet produce a stable complete
partition. No labels informed clustering, no group count was supplied, and no
mutation was published. All 82 infrastructure tests passed.

M11.27 tests the motivating accumulation hypothesis with 250 rather than 100
observations per disjoint class slice. The proxy-only grid retained normalized
raw MobileNet space and HDBSCAN, selecting a 20% flag budget and minimum group
size 5. Untouched accumulated event recall remained 100%, but distinct-group
recall fell from 61.1% to 50.0%, adjusted Rand index remained low at 0.049,
and purity was 77.5%. Three of nine cells recovered both unseen categories,
three recovered one, and three recovered neither. The larger buffer averaged
exactly two unknown-majority groups, but their membership was not consistently
aligned with the two semantic classes. More repeated observations therefore
improve density support but are not sufficient when ranking bias and feature
overlap shape which observations enter each group. No labels informed
clustering, no group count was supplied, and no mutation was published.

M11.28 audits prior art for automatic partitioning of accumulated unsupported
observations. Cluster-based stream novelty detection, MINAS, and related
concept-evolution work already establish the buffer-persist-cluster lifecycle;
DenStream and HDBSCAN establish evolving and variable-density clustering;
FINCH supplies a group-count-free hierarchy; and AutoNovel, GCD, UNO, ORCA,
DeepCluster, and SCAN show that representation objectives and seen-class bias
can dominate semantic partition quality. COBRAS provides a precedent for
turning later human feedback into pairwise partition constraints. The plan now
prioritizes a frozen-embedding FINCH baseline, followed by isolated streaming
memory, group-count estimation, representation-learning, and feedback studies.
Accordingly, buffering and autoclustering unknown observations is no longer a
candidate novelty claim. GEODE's open research claim is limited to integration
with versioned support profiles, stable review provenance, editable ellipsoid
graphs, and confirmation-gated transactional adaptation and rollback. This was
a literature and attribution milestone; it produced no experimental gain and
changed no model behavior.

M11.29 implements FINCH first-neighbor hierarchy clustering without a supplied
group count or distance radius, then compares it with DBSCAN and HDBSCAN under
identical normalized MobileNet embeddings, flag budgets, and labels-hidden
grouping. Proxy classes selected 30% flagging and HDBSCAN minimum group size 3.
The strongest FINCH candidate used hierarchy level 0 and 20% flagging: it also
reached 100% proxy distinct-group recall, but its adjusted Rand index was 0.176
versus 0.251 for selected HDBSCAN and its review burden was 15.28 versus 8.33
reviews per 1,000. FINCH therefore did not clear the development selection
gate. The frozen HDBSCAN policy reproduced M11.26 on untouched classes 8/9:
100% accumulated event recall, 61.1% distinct-group recall, adjusted Rand index
0.059, and 79.8% purity. This confirms that removing radius and group-count
inputs does not resolve representation overlap or review fragmentation. No
final labels informed selection, no semantic IDs were emitted, and no mutation
was published. All 83 infrastructure tests passed.

M11.30 adds a bounded DenStream-style fading micro-cluster memory with stable
review IDs and a MINAS-style geometric lifecycle distinction between
`known_extension` and `emerging_novel`. Neither relation creates a semantic
class or publishes a mutation. Proxy classes selected assignment radius 0.75
and zero fading from 12 streaming candidates in normalized MobileNet space.
On untouched classes 8/9, accumulated event recall remained 100%, but
distinct-group recall fell to 5.6% from the matched M11.29 HDBSCAN baseline's
61.1%, noise-inclusive adjusted Rand index fell to 0.013 from 0.059, and
purity fell to 48.6% from 79.8%. Review burden decreased from 7.22 to 3.33 per
1,000 observations, but duplicate review rate increased from 66.8% to 74.2%.
Geometric lifecycle relation accuracy was 66.7%; this is a diagnostic against
held-out labels, not evidence of autonomous semantic novelty recognition.
Streaming memory therefore failed the advancement gate: persistence and stable
provenance did not repair representation overlap or group merging. Final labels
did not inform policy selection, no temporary semantic IDs were emitted, and no
mutation was published.

M11.31 adds deterministic K-means whose group count is selected without labels
by maximum silhouette score over a bounded candidate range. Held-out proxy
classes selected a maximum count of five; that bound was frozen before final
classes were opened. The estimator inferred 2.33 raw groups on average in both
proxy and final cells: six of nine final cells produced two groups and three
produced three, against two evaluation categories. On untouched classes 8/9,
noise-inclusive adjusted Rand index increased to 0.449 from the matched M11.29
HDBSCAN baseline's 0.059, while accumulated event recall remained 100% and
review burden decreased from 7.22 to 5.00 per 1,000 observations. However,
distinct-group recall fell from 61.1% to 38.9%, purity fell from 79.8% to
65.6%, and duplicate review rate increased from 66.8% to 77.2%. The estimator
therefore failed the predeclared advancement gate despite its ARI gain. Proxy
labels were used only to select the estimator bound and were not consumed by
clustering; final labels remained observational, no semantic IDs were emitted,
and no mutation was published.

M11.32 tests a versioned transductive linear reconstruction control: PCA is fit
without labels to the union of known and currently unlabeled normalized
MobileNet features, projected to 32 components, and L2-normalized before the
frozen HDBSCAN review policy runs. The transform has access to unlabeled final
features but no final labels, giving the control a favorable transductive
setting. On untouched classes 8/9, accumulated event recall remained 100%, but
distinct-group recall fell to 16.7% from the source representation's 61.1%,
noise-inclusive adjusted Rand index fell to 0.014 from 0.059, and purity fell
to 66.3% from 79.8%. Review burden increased from 7.22 to 7.50 per 1,000 and
duplicate review rate increased from 66.8% to 73.2%. Linear reconstruction of
joint variance therefore does not supply the semantic partition objective
motivated by category-discovery methods. The experimental feature model is
identified as `joint-pca-l2-32-v1`; cached source features and production
representations were not modified, no semantic IDs were emitted, and no model
mutation was published.

M11.33 records bounded must-link and cannot-link constraints only after an
ordinary proxy review exists, then learns a deterministic diagonal metric from
within-link and between-link feature differences. Each episode freezes that
metric before clustering untouched final classes. Proxy reviews supplied 128.4
must-links and 209.9 cannot-links per final cell on average. Relative to the
M11.29 HDBSCAN reference, final distinct-group recall remained 61.1%, adjusted
Rand index improved from 0.059 to 0.082, purity improved from 79.8% to 82.4%,
and review burden fell from 7.22 to 6.39 per 1,000 observations. However,
duplicate review rate increased from 66.8% to 72.6%, so the transferable metric
failed the full advancement gate. This is a metric-learning precursor to, not
an implementation of, COBRAS-style partition refinement. Constraints used
only reviewed proxy classes 6/7; final classes 8/9 remained observational, no
semantic IDs were emitted, and no representation or model mutation was
published.

M11.34 applies delayed feedback directly to the reviewed proxy partition. A
deterministic COBRAS-style control splits clusters containing cannot-linked
must-link components and merges fragments connected by must-links. Relative to
unconstrained proxy HDBSCAN, adjusted Rand index increased from 0.251 to 0.273,
purity increased from 90.0% to 91.0%, duplicate review rate fell from 74.9% to
70.9%, and mean final proxy partition count contracted from 4.67 to 4.00.
Distinct-group and accumulated event recall remained 100%. Review burden rose
slightly from 8.33 to 8.61 per 1,000 observations. The control consumed 128.4
must-links and 209.9 cannot-links per cell on average, so it establishes that
post-review split/merge feedback can improve a partition, not that the current
query burden is practical. Final classes received no constraints and reproduced
the unchanged M11.29 observational reference. No semantic IDs or mutation were
published.

M11.35 freezes the M11.34 split/merge operation and compares answer-blind active
queries, deterministic random queries, and dense feedback at identical proxy
partitions. Active selection alternates farthest within-cluster pairs, which
target splits, with nearest across-cluster pairs, which target merges; a test
confirms selected pair identities do not change when hidden answers are
flipped. Eight active queries raised proxy adjusted Rand index from 0.2511 to
0.2654 and reduced duplicate review rate from 74.95% to 73.11%. Random eight
reached 0.2635 ARI and 74.05% duplicates. Active eight delivered 0.00178 ARI
gain and 0.00230 duplicate reduction per query, versus dense feedback's
0.000064 and 0.000120. Dense feedback still achieved the strongest duplicate
rate at 70.90% and ARI 0.2729, but required 338.3 queries on average. Active
eight is therefore the retained assisted-efficiency point, not an autonomous
partition policy. Final classes remained unconstrained and observational; no
semantic IDs or mutation were published.

M11.36 fixes the active budget at eight proxy queries and independently varies
missing, flipped, and explicitly contradictory answers. With 25% and 50%
missing answers, adjusted Rand index remained 0.2636 and 0.2628 and duplicate
review rate remained 73.74% and 73.58%, respectively. Flipping one of eight
answers reduced ARI to 0.2393, below the no-feedback baseline of 0.2511;
flipping two reached only 0.2438. These isolated wrong answers were internally
consistent and therefore undetectable by graph consistency alone. In contrast,
the validator detected and quarantined every batch containing one or two
explicit opposite answers, returning the unchanged baseline partition instead
of applying inconsistent constraints. Consistency validation is therefore a
necessary preflight control but is insufficient for answer reliability.
Constraint persistence remains disabled, final classes remained observational,
and no semantic IDs or mutation were published.

M11.37 obtains two independently perturbed answers for each of the same eight
proxy pair identities and accepts a constraint only when both answers match.
At 12.5% error per respondent, confirmation accepted six constraints, abstained
on 25%, accepted no harmful constraints, retained ARI of 0.2656, and reduced
duplicate review rate to 72.85%. This required 16 responses rather than eight.
At 25% error, confirmation accepted 4.67 constraints on average and abstained
on 41.67%, but independent double errors survived in 33.3% of cells. Confirmed
ARI was 0.2509, near the 0.2511 no-feedback baseline and above the matched
single-answer result of 0.2463, while duplicate rate was 74.32%. Independent
confirmation therefore mitigates isolated answer errors but does not establish
reliability at the higher tested error rate. Constraint persistence remains
disabled, final classes remained observational, and no semantic IDs or mutation
were published.

M11.38 repeats independent Bernoulli answer corruption 50 times in each of nine
proxy cells, yielding 450 paired observations per error rate. Confirmation's
mean ARI delta over each cell's no-feedback partition was +0.0143 at zero error,
+0.0135 at 5%, +0.0108 at 12.5%, and +0.0055 at 25%. Harmful constraints were
accepted in 0%, 1.78%, 10.44%, and 37.78% of observations, respectively. The
fifth-percentile ARI delta was zero at 0% and 5% error, then negative at -0.0113
and -0.0229. Even clean confirmation had a fifth-percentile purity delta of
-0.0389 and a 95th-percentile duplicate-rate increase of +0.0256. The
predeclared lower-tail gate therefore failed at every tested error rate despite
positive mean outcomes. Pairwise feedback remains an assisted analysis tool;
constraints must not be persisted or used for autonomous mutation. Final
classes remained observational and no semantic IDs were published.

M11.39 adds a separately versioned ImageNet-1K ResNet-18 global-average-pooled
representation and compares it with MobileNetV2 under the unchanged M11.29
ranking, flagging, HDBSCAN, source-slice, and evaluation protocol. On proxy
classes, ResNet-18 reduced reviews from 8.33 to 6.39 per 1,000 and duplicate
rate from 74.95% to 72.84%, but distinct-group recall fell from 100% to 83.33%,
ARI from 0.2511 to 0.1995, and purity from 90.02% to 85.13%. It therefore failed
the proxy gate. The untouched final observation was also worse on distinct
recall (27.78% versus 61.11%), ARI (0.0552 versus 0.0592), purity (69.28% versus
79.83%), and duplicate rate (68.35% versus 66.76%). MobileNetV2 remains the
retained representation. No pairwise feedback, final-label selection, semantic
IDs, or mutation were used.

M11.40 executes a closure audit over all M11.29-M11.39 artifacts. Every artifact
declares final labels observational and mutation unpublished; feedback studies
also declare constraints unpersisted and final partitions unrefined. The audit
therefore retains only the M11.29 normalized-MobileNet, 30%-flag, HDBSCAN-size-3
policy for automatic review grouping. Later grouping, representation, and
feedback variants remain analysis-only. The M12 baseline freezes all compatible
classes as candidates, exact class-SDF argmin as the agreement reference, and
the existing seven routing counters. M12 must demonstrate sublinear candidate
growth, at least 99% exhaustive agreement, bounded open-set change, and explicit
resource budgets on toy scaling before real-feature confirmation.

M12.0 sweeps classes, dimensions, primitives per class, and batch size
independently on deterministic synthetic ellipsoid models. All exhaustive
counter invariants passed: compatible, shortlisted, exact-class, and
materialized-score pairs equal $N C$, while primitive evaluations equal
$N C K$. Median latency log-log slope was 1.003 with class count and 1.008 with
primitives per class; the corresponding operation-count slopes were exactly
1.0. Snapshot bytes scaled with slopes 0.998 in class count and 1.102 in
dimension. At 64 classes, two primitives, four dimensions, and batch 64, p50,
p95, and p99 latency were 1.454, 1.559, and 1.579 ms, median throughput was
44,020 samples/s, canonical snapshot size was 39,063 bytes, and observed process
peak working set was about 37.8 MB. These are hardware-specific measurements,
not universal timing laws. Shortlisting remained disabled.

M12.1 orders classes by conservative support-sphere lower bounds and stops only
when the next bound exceeds the best exact class score. Across 4-256 classes,
three seeds, and 256 points per cell, prediction agreement with exhaustive
routing was 100% and maximum winning-score error was zero. Mean evaluated
candidates grew with log-log slope 0.417, while shortlist latency slope was
0.492 versus exhaustive latency slope 1.069. At 256 classes the router evaluated
16.80 classes per sample, 6.56% of exhaustive candidates, and one cell reduced
exact class pairs from 65,536 to 4,230. The unoptimized per-sample Python path
still took 129.3 ms versus 12.8 ms exhaustive at that scale, so it does not yet
provide a net latency benefit and is not bound to production inference.

M12.2 groups active sample-class pairs by class within each bound-order round.
It preserved 100% exhaustive agreement, zero winning-score error, and the same
0.417 candidate-count slope. At four classes it was 19.4 times faster than the
scalar shortlist, and at 64 classes it remained 1.67 times faster. Repeated
class calls across rounds caused the benefit to disappear by 256 classes, where
batched routing took 108.6 ms, scalar routing 99.5 ms, and exhaustive routing
9.6 ms. Batched latency slope was 1.180 and no exhaustive break-even point was
observed. The implementation remains experimental and unbound.

M12.3 initializes each sample from its lowest-bound class, then evaluates every
remaining class at most once on the batch subset for which its bound can still
beat the incumbent. Across independent class, dimension, primitive, and batch
sweeps, agreement remained 100% and maximum winning-score error was
$1.78\times10^{-15}$. The global class schedule weakened class-count pruning:
candidate slope rose to 0.924, although routed latency slope remained 0.909
versus 1.007 exhaustive. No condition broke even. The best measured speed ratio
was 0.87 at batch size one; at 256 classes the router evaluated 71.8% of classes
and took 22.3 ms versus 13.3 ms exhaustive. Class-major routing is rejected as
a latency optimization and remains unbound.

M12.4 prebuilds a nearest-neighbor index over class support centroids, exact
scores fixed top-$k$ proposals, and checks every omitted class's conservative
support-sphere lower bound. If any omitted bound can beat the proposal winner,
all omitted classes are scored. Agreement remained 100% with zero winning-score
error, but fallback was 100% for the best measured conditions and candidate
slopes were 1.000-1.007 across budgets. There was no break-even; the best speed
ratio was 0.564 at 256 classes with $k=1$. Approximate proposal lookup is
therefore rejected as an exact CPU acceleration under the current bounds.

M12.5 greedily removes the primitive with the smallest maximum class-score
contribution on an unlabeled calibration set. Every tentative removal is rolled
back if calibration prediction agreement falls below 99% or score drift exceeds
0.25. Across 4, 8, and 16 primitives per class, retained models used 64.5-90.6%
of primitives and delivered 1.09-1.53x inference speedups. These gains did not
generalize: worst-seed held-out agreement was only 92.2-95.7%, and maximum score
drift was 0.454-1.073. No condition passed the predeclared 99% agreement and 0.30
score-drift exit gate. Calibration-only primitive pruning is rejected.

M12.6 adds a disjoint 512-sample confirmation stream to every tentative removal
and evaluates once on 1,024 untouched samples. The smallest growth condition
retained 98.4% of primitives, reduced serialized bytes by 1.5%, and reached
98.93% worst-seed final agreement with 0.268 maximum score drift. Larger growth
conditions reached only 94.43-96.68% agreement despite 1.15-1.42x speedups.
No condition met the frozen 99% final agreement gate. Confirmation-gated
compression remains unbound.

M12.7 executes the complete M12 exit audit. Cost instrumentation, counter
invariants, sublinear scalar candidate growth (slope 0.417), and exhaustive
agreement (100%) pass. Net routing latency fails: maximum speed ratios were
0.099 scalar, 0.870 class-major, and 0.564 certified top-$k$, with no batched
break-even. Certified lookup candidate slopes were 1.000-1.007 because fallback
was effectively exhaustive. Confirmed compression peaked at 98.93% lower-tail
agreement, below the frozen 99% gate. Real-feature advancement is blocked by
the failed toy operating-envelope gates. M12 is complete with no efficient
scalable-routing claim, no bound candidate router, and no bound compression
policy; exhaustive exact class SDF remains authoritative over the measured
range.

## M13: primitive-family and subtractive-SDF reopening

M13.1 compares full-covariance, diagonal-covariance, and trace-matched spherical
primitives without changing Tier 4 splits, transforms, candidate budgets,
fusion, calibration, or multinomial readout. The five-seed CPU screen selected
the sphere on four seeds. Mean test accuracy was 84.00% sphere, 83.45% diagonal,
and 81.08% full. Paired sphere-minus-diagonal accuracy was +0.55 pp with a
seed-level 95% $t$ interval of [0.046, 1.054] pp; sphere-minus-full was +2.925 pp
[2.160, 3.690]. The sphere used 115.2 primitives on average versus 107.2
diagonal and 58.6 full. Four accidentally repeated runs were retained in the
append-only source log; deduplication by experiment ID found identical
scientific metrics outside runtime measurements.

M13.2 makes primitive identity explicit in construction and output. Messages
now report `sphere`, `diagonal ellipsoid`, or `ellipsoid`, including subtractive
rounds. OpenCL kernels on AMD `gfx1201` fit batched sphere and diagonal centers
and radii directly; full covariance retains host eigendecomposition while
candidate scoring, fused inference, and CSG run on OpenCL. Family projection
preserves equal sphere radii and identity orientation after subtractive nudge.
The complete gate passed 164 tests in 12.790 seconds plus four dedicated real-
OpenCL parity tests.

The definitive five-seed GPU matrix evaluated A0 additive, A1 validation-gated
excision, and A2 active repair for every family. Sphere A0 reached 83.80%,
diagonal 83.34%, and full 81.46%. Sphere exceeded diagonal by +0.463 pp with a
paired 95% interval of [0.080, 0.845] pp and full by +2.338 pp [1.095, 3.580].
Sphere and diagonal accepted no subtractive primitives. Full accepted nine,
changed 11 predictions, and moved accuracy by -0.025 pp with interval
[-0.068, 0.018], while slightly worsening NLL. A2 added no candidates.

This supports a spherical Tier 4 candidate, not a universal replacement for
ellipsoids. The GPU and CPU protocols use different candidate-generation paths,
so their absolute values are not pooled. Subtractive SDF remains disabled by
default for all three families because reopening it produced no gain.

## M14: probabilistic field semantics

M14.1 adds a probability interpretation without changing the GEODE constructor
or its general geometric interface. A covariance-fitted primitive with radii
$r_i=\sqrt{d\lambda_i}$ is interpreted as a Gaussian with covariance
$R\,\mathrm{diag}(r_i^2/d)R^T$. Primitive negative log likelihoods are fused
with stable negative log-mean-exp, first within each expert and then across
experts. CPU and AMD `gfx1201` OpenCL implementations agree within float32
tolerance. Empty class models and subtractive primitives fail closed because
neither defines the required normalized class density; geometric CSG is not
silently reinterpreted as density subtraction. Six focused probability tests
and the complete 174-test regression gate passed.

M14.2 fits each full, diagonal, and spherical model once per seed, then scores
that same frozen model under geometric normalized-radial and probabilistic
Gaussian-mixture semantics. Splits, transforms, candidate budgets, calibration
samples, and five seeds `[11,23,37,53,71]` match M13.2. Probabilistic fitting was
not used, so this isolates inference semantics rather than testing a
likelihood-trained constructor.

Raw probabilistic scores improved NLL and ECE for every family, but did not
improve accuracy. Sphere raw NLL fell from 1.4720 to 0.7374 and ECE from 0.5756
to 0.1069, while accuracy moved from 82.94% to 82.83%. After multinomial
calibration, sphere NLL improved from 0.4847 to 0.4772 and ECE from 0.0344 to
0.0244, while accuracy moved from 83.80% to 83.65% (-0.15 pp). Diagonal
multinomial accuracy moved -0.64 pp and full moved -1.55 pp; full temperature
NLL also worsened by 0.0764. Shared scikit-learn readouts emitted iteration-
limit warnings on some fits, so calibrated differences remain research
evidence rather than a production advancement claim.

The evidence supports retaining probabilistic sphere scores as an optional
calibration/uncertainty view. It does not support replacing geometric scoring,
does not establish OOD improvement, and does not define subtractive probability
semantics. A future causal test would fit mixture weights and covariance
parameters by held-out likelihood while preserving the architecture's
primitive interface and non-probabilistic task flexibility.

## M15: hybrid field readout

M15.0 makes the next comparison auditable before introducing a hybrid model.
Multinomial, diagonal, and feature-logistic inputs are now standardized using
statistics fitted only on the calibration fold. Multinomial and feature
readouts retain those statistics for inference; diagonal coefficients are
mapped back to the original score coordinates. Raw and temperature semantics
are unchanged.

Every newly generated result row now records whether classifier inputs were
standardized, optimizer iterations, the iteration limit, captured convergence
warnings, and an explicit convergence flag. A forced one-iteration test proves
that optimizer failure is retained rather than emitted and lost as a process
warning. Seven focused readout tests and the complete 177-test regression gate
passed in 11.705 seconds. This is an engineering prerequisite with no accuracy
or calibration improvement claim. Existing M14 artifacts remain unchanged;
M15.1 will refit all compared readouts under the new matched protocol.

M15.1 fits one spherical model per seed and reuses it for geometric,
probabilistic, and concatenated hybrid inputs. Each of the four readouts is fit
once on the disjoint calibration fold and reused unchanged for selection and
test. All 40 result rows converged. Selection chose hybrid on three seeds and
probabilistic-only on two; test data remained observational.

Across `[11,23,37,53,71]`, hybrid reached 83.875% mean accuracy and 0.46750
NLL. Geometric reached 83.950% and 0.47044; probabilistic reached 83.9875% and
0.46901. Hybrid therefore improved NLL by 0.00294 versus geometric and 0.00151
versus probabilistic, while accuracy moved -0.075 pp and -0.1125 pp. Both
accuracy changes are within the predeclared 0.25 pp tolerance, so the aggregate
gate passes narrowly and authorizes only M15.2 frozen-geometry likelihood
optimization. The feature-logistic control remained stronger at 83.975%
accuracy and 0.46538 NLL, so this is not evidence that hybrid geometry adds
predictive value beyond the transformed representation. The complete 179-test
gate passed in 11.602 seconds.

M15.2a adds one positive global covariance temperature to the primitive
Gaussian NLL while retaining the exact M15.1 sphere models and data protocol.
The scalar is optimized by conditional NLL on calibration data only; final
test labels remain observational. CPU analytic tests and real OpenCL parity at
non-unit temperature passed.

Across `[11,23,37,53,71]`, fitted temperatures ranged from 2.278 to 2.519,
with mean 2.397 and standard deviation 0.090. Tuned probabilistic NLL improved
on all five seeds, from 0.46901 to 0.46749 on average; accuracy moved -0.05 pp
to 83.9375%. Tuned hybrid improved mean accuracy by 0.0375 pp to 83.9125% and
NLL by 0.00044 to 0.46705. All likelihood optimizers and readouts converged.
The frozen accuracy/NLL gate therefore passes and authorizes only per-class
covariance temperature. The feature-only control remains stronger at 83.975%
accuracy and 0.46538 NLL, and temperature fitting does not establish geometric
value beyond the transformed representation. The complete 182-test regression
gate passed in 11.724 seconds.

M15.2b initializes one positive temperature per class from the M15.2a global
optimum and jointly minimizes calibration conditional NLL. CPU and real OpenCL
inference agree for unequal class temperatures. Geometry, splits, readouts,
seeds, and final-test handling remain identical to M15.2a.

Only seeds 11 and 37 moved beyond the global scalar; seeds 23, 53, and 71
retained it exactly. Mean calibration NLL improved by 0.00725, but the gain did
not transfer. Per-class hybrid accuracy remained 83.9125% and NLL worsened by
0.000034 to 0.46709 relative to global temperature. Per-class probabilistic
accuracy fell 0.0125 pp to 83.9250% and NLL worsened by 0.000070 to 0.46756.
All optimizers and readouts converged. Neither accuracy nor NLL improved for
the primary hybrid comparison, so the predeclared gate fails. Nonnegative
mixture weights and combined parameters were not run, as required by the
increasing-freedom protocol. M15 closes with global covariance temperature as
the maximum supported likelihood freedom; feature logistic remains the best
tested control. The complete 184-test regression gate passed in 11.747 seconds.

## E0.1: end-to-end runtime foundations

E0.1 begins the end-to-end implementation without changing any fitted model or
published inference path. Versioned frozen schemas now cover run, stage,
checkpoint, and metric records. The run contract makes the four preflight P0
requirements executable: calibration partitions must be distinct, pretraining
provenance is explicit, geometry capacity is bounded, model-selection and final
domains are disjoint, and replay identity cannot silently use numerical
tolerances.

The geometry-feasibility probe evaluates every class before fitting. It records
sample count, transformed dimension, effective rank, conditioning, and
parameter/sample ratio for sphere, axis-aligned, shrinkage, low-rank diagonal,
and full families. Focused tests confirm that a sparse class can retain a simple
family while full geometry is rejected, and that collapsed or non-finite
representations fail closed.

Local durability primitives now provide atomic partial-directory publication,
content hashes, `SUCCESS` markers, idempotent retry, tamper detection, an
append-only metric ledger, and explicit JSON plus non-pickled NumPy
checkpoints. A Windows-specific read-only `fsync` failure was found by the
checkpoint test and corrected by flushing through writable descriptors. A
second hardening test found that permissive path checks admitted glob
metacharacters; portable path-component allowlists now protect stage,
checkpoint, and array names.

The complete focused gate passed 19 tests in 0.220 seconds, including the three
pre-existing canonical-manifest tests. This is a correctness milestone, not an
accuracy result. E0 is complete. E1 and E2 remain partial until a Tier 4 smoke
episode demonstrates feature/class-stage resume, model-specific RNG and
optimizer restoration, and interrupted-versus-uninterrupted equivalence.

## E1.1: Tier 4 resumable recovery smoke

E1.1 integrates the existing Tier 4 HOG, PCA/LDA, GEODE fitter, and raw-score
path with immutable feature, transform, per-class, and assembly stages. Every
class model is serialized as explicit centers, radii, orientations, polarities,
and mixture alpha rather than as a pickled process object. A retry reuses a
committed stage only when its input hashes match.

The focused synthetic test interrupts after the class-0 commit, then resumes
the same attempt. Feature extraction runs once, class 0 is not refit, each
class is fit exactly once across both executions, and the resumed assembly
hashes equal an uninterrupted run. The complete runtime, legacy research
infrastructure, and recovery regression gate passed 115 tests in 9.666 seconds;
Pylance reported no diagnostics and Python compilation succeeded.

The public-data qualification used 3,500 deterministic CIFAR-10 HOG examples,
seed 42, 32 requested PCA components, and one RANSAC iteration as a cheap
recovery exercise. After an injected failure following class 0, resumed and
uninterrupted runs produced identical summary, validation-prediction, and
validation-score hashes. Raw validation accuracy was 35.55% on 526 examples;
this deliberately underfit smoke value is not a predictive-performance claim.
The final split retained 700 frozen indices, but transformed final-test features
and labels were not materialized or evaluated.

This closes the Tier 4 class-boundary recovery slice, not E1 or E2. A local
executor, status/resume CLI, mid-shard feature failure, and iterative optimizer,
RNG, sampler, and epoch-history restoration remain required.

## E1.2: local executor and resume status

E1.2 adds an ordered `LocalExecutor` over the immutable artifact store. Status
inspection verifies committed output hashes and distinguishes pending, partial,
committed, and corrupt stages. Resume removes an abandoned partial directory,
reuses only verified predecessors with matching input hashes, and executes the
remaining stage specifications in order. Tier 4 routes every stage commit
through this executor and exposes `--status` without feature extraction plus an
explicit `--resume` mode.

The focused executor test injected a geometry-writer failure, observed a
committed predecessor and partial failed stage, resumed without rerunning the
predecessor, and then detected deliberate output corruption. The complete gate
passed 117 tests in 8.308 seconds with clean Pylance, syntax, compilation, and
editor diagnostics.

The public 3,500-sample CIFAR-10 qualification interrupted after class 0. Its
status snapshot reported features, transform, and class 0 committed with
assembly pending. Resume called the feature loader once in total, completed 13
verified stages, retained one metric event, and reproduced all three E1.1
assembly hashes exactly. Final-test data remained unobserved. E1 stays partial
until failure during feature writing or shard extraction is qualified.

## E1.3: feature-stage interruption recovery

E1.3 injects failure inside the feature writer after `features.npy` is flushed
but before labels, manifest, `SUCCESS`, or atomic publication. Status reports
the feature stage as partial and transform/assembly as pending. Retry removes
the abandoned partial directory, executes feature extraction again, and then
uses the ordinary verified executor path.

The focused synthetic test produced the same assembly hashes as an
uninterrupted run. The full runtime, research-infrastructure, and Tier 4 gate
passed 118 tests in 8.558 seconds with clean Pylance, compilation, and editor
diagnostics.

The public qualification used the same 3,500-sample CIFAR-10 HOG configuration
and frozen E1.1 hash oracle. The failed and resumed attempt called feature
extraction twice, completed 13 stages, retained one metric event, and matched
the summary, validation-prediction, and validation-score hashes exactly. The
final test remained unobserved. This passes the feature-stage half of the E1
gate. Failure inside an unpublished class fit remained to be qualified rather
than inferred from E1.1's interruption after a committed class boundary.

## E1.4: in-class interruption recovery

E1.4 extends `--fail-during` to a `class-<id>` stage. The failure fires after
the fitter returns but before explicit model serialization, manifest creation,
or publication. The synthetic test observed committed features and transform,
a partial class 0, and class seeds `[7, 7, 8]`: failed class 0, retried class 0,
then class 1. Resume did not reload features and matched an uninterrupted
assembly.

The public qualification used the frozen 3,500-sample CIFAR-10 configuration.
Its interrupted status showed features and transform committed, class 0
partial, and assembly pending. Resume used one feature extraction total,
completed 13 stages, retained one metric event, and matched all three E1.1
hashes. The final test remained unobserved. The full gate passed 119 tests in
9.018 seconds with clean Pylance, compilation, and editor diagnostics. E1 is
complete based on failures inside both required stage types.

Independent feature shards remain desirable for large datasets but are a scale
optimization, not required to establish local interruption correctness.

## E2.1: exact refinement checkpoint state

E2.1 gives `SDFOptimizer` a stable explicit state contract. Class, expert, and
ellipsoid positions replace process-specific object IDs. JSON records class
order, topology, optimizer hyperparameters, score scales, and which momentum
buffers exist; non-pickled NumPy arrays hold centers, radii, orientations, and
center/precision momentum. Restore rejects mismatched class topology and NumPy
bit-generator type.

`RefinementCheckpointAdapter` adds RNG state, sampler progress, epoch/global
step, and the full epoch-history prefix to the existing atomic checkpoint
store. The focused qualification trained a real two-class `SDFOptimizer` for
four epochs, interrupted after epoch 2/step 4, restored into fresh model and
optimizer objects, and continued to epoch 4/step 8. All four history records,
optimizer metadata, and ten geometry/momentum arrays matched uninterrupted
execution exactly. The broader runtime, research-infrastructure, E1, and Tier 6
gate passed 156 tests in 9.796 seconds with clean syntax, compilation, and
editor diagnostics.

This is a recoverability result, not a predictive-performance claim. E2 stays
partial until the adapter is wired into the production Tier 6 epoch loop and
representation/Torch optimizer plus distributed sampler state are qualified.

## E2.2: Tier 6 production epoch resume

E2.2 wires the explicit adapter into `supervised_refinement`. Each completed
epoch now commits model/optimizer arrays, NumPy generator state, deterministic
sampler iteration and epoch, global step, score scales, and the complete history
prefix. At the final epoch of each refinement iteration, scale recomputation is
performed before checkpoint publication so a boundary resume cannot skip that
state transition. Restored sampler progress must agree with checkpoint epoch
metadata.

The production-loop qualification used one four-epoch fixed refinement set and
injected failure after the epoch-2 checkpoint. A fresh model resumed through
the same public function, produced four valid checkpoints, and ended at epoch
4/step 12. All four history rows, score scales, and six final center/radius/
orientation arrays matched uninterrupted execution exactly. The expanded gate
passed 157 tests in 9.583 seconds with clean compilation and editor diagnostics.

This remains recoverability evidence, not a predictive-performance result. E2
is partial until representation/Torch optimizer and distributed sampler state
are covered and a local history export/dashboard path is available.

## E2.3: explicit Torch representation state

E2.3 adds `RepresentationCheckpointAdapter` at the Torch framework boundary.
It serializes model weights and buffers, optimizer state, optional scheduler
and gradient-scaler state, CPU/CUDA Torch RNG, NumPy RNG, sampler metadata, and
complete epoch history into explicit JSON plus raw-byte NumPy arrays. No pickle
or process-specific parameter identity is persisted. Restore validates Torch
version, named model topology, optimizer parameter-group topology, optional
component presence, and NumPy bit-generator type.

The qualification trained a small stochastic Dropout representation with Adam
and StepLR for four epochs. It checkpointed after epoch 2/step 4, restored into
fresh objects initialized with different seeds, and continued to epoch 4/step 8. All four loss records, four model tensors, Adam moment/step state, and the
scheduler matched uninterrupted execution exactly. The checkpoint contained 17
explicit arrays. The expanded gate passed 159 tests in 10.918 seconds with
clean compilation and editor diagnostics.

This is adapter recoverability evidence, not a trained-representation or
predictive-performance claim. E2 remains partial until the adapter is wired
into a production representation loop and distributed sampler state plus local
history export/dashboard are qualified.

## E2.4: production representation epoch resume

E2.4 adds `src.runtime.train_representation` as the reusable Torch training
boundary. It owns deterministic epoch permutations, optimizer and optional
scheduler/scaler updates, complete history records, epoch checkpoint
publication, restore validation, and a post-checkpoint failure hook. The
representation checkpoint adapter remains the serialization authority.

The qualification trained the same stochastic Dropout representation through
an uninterrupted four-epoch path and a production path interrupted after the
epoch-2 checkpoint. Resume into fresh model, Adam, scheduler, and RNG objects
started at epoch 2 and reached epoch 4/step 8. Model tensors, optimizer state,
scheduler state, and all four history records matched exactly; four checkpoints
were committed and the final checkpoint retained 17 explicit arrays. This is
recovery evidence, not a predictive-performance claim. E2 remains partial only
for distributed sampler state and a local history export/dashboard path. The
